Original: with my_customers as (  select distinct c_customer_sk         , c_current_addr_sk  from         ( select cs_sold_date_sk sold_date_sk,                  cs_bill_customer_sk customer_sk,                  cs_item_sk item_sk,                  cs_wholesale_cost wholesale_cost           from   catalog_sales           union all           select ws_sold_date_sk sold_date_sk,                  ws_bill_customer_sk customer_sk,                  ws_item_sk item_sk,                  ws_wholesale_cost wholesale_cost           from   web_sales          ) cs_or_ws_sales,          item,          date_dim,          customer  where   sold_date_sk = d_date_sk          and item_sk = i_item_sk          and i_category = 'Electronics'          and i_class = 'portable'          and c_customer_sk = cs_or_ws_sales.customer_sk          and d_moy = 6          and d_year = 2002          and wholesale_cost BETWEEN 70 AND 100          and c_birth_year BETWEEN 1978 AND 1991  )  , my_revenue as (  select c_customer_sk,         sum(ss_ext_sales_price) as revenue  from   my_customers,         store_sales,         customer_address,         store,         date_dim  where  c_current_addr_sk = ca_address_sk         and ca_county = s_county         and ca_state = s_state         and ss_sold_date_sk = d_date_sk         and c_customer_sk = ss_customer_sk         and ss_wholesale_cost BETWEEN 70 AND 100         and s_state in ('AL','AR','FL'                     ,'MN','MO','NC'                     ,'NY','OK','PA'                     ,'WI')         and d_month_seq between (select distinct d_month_seq+1                                  from   date_dim where d_year = 2002 and d_moy = 6)                            and  (select distinct d_month_seq+3                                  from   date_dim where d_year = 2002 and d_moy = 6)  group by c_customer_sk  )  , segments as  (select cast((revenue/50) as int) as segment   from   my_revenue  )   select  segment, count(*) as num_customers, segment*50 as segment_base  from segments  group by segment  order by segment, num_customers  limit 100;
Rewritten: with all_sales as (
    select cs_sold_date_sk as sold_date_sk,
           cs_bill_customer_sk as customer_sk,
           cs_item_sk as item_sk,
           cs_wholesale_cost as wholesale_cost
    from catalog_sales
    union all
    select ws_sold_date_sk,
           ws_bill_customer_sk,
           ws_item_sk,
           ws_wholesale_cost
    from web_sales
),
qualifying_customers as (
    select distinct s.customer_sk
    from all_sales s
    join item i on i.i_item_sk = s.item_sk
    join date_dim d on d.d_date_sk = s.sold_date_sk
    where i.i_category = 'Electronics'
      and i.i_class = 'portable'
      and d.d_year = 2002
      and d.d_moy = 6
      and s.wholesale_cost between 70 and 100
),
my_customers as (
    select c.c_customer_sk, c.c_current_addr_sk
    from customer c
    join qualifying_customers qc on qc.customer_sk = c.c_customer_sk
    where c.c_birth_year between 1978 and 1991
),
my_revenue as (
    select c.c_customer_sk,
           sum(ss.ss_ext_sales_price) as revenue
    from my_customers c
    join store_sales ss on ss.ss_customer_sk = c.c_customer_sk
    join customer_address ca on ca.ca_address_sk = c.c_current_addr_sk
    join store s on s.s_county = ca.ca_county and s.s_state = ca.ca_state
    join date_dim d on d.d_date_sk = ss.ss_sold_date_sk
    where ss.ss_wholesale_cost between 70 and 100
      and s.s_state in ('AL','AR','FL','MN','MO','NC','NY','OK','PA','WI')
      and ca.ca_state in ('AL','AR','FL','MN','MO','NC','NY','OK','PA','WI')
      and d.d_year = 2002
      and d.d_moy between 7 and 9
    group by c.c_customer_sk
),
segments as (
    select cast(revenue / 50 as int) as segment
    from my_revenue
)
select segment,
       count(*) as num_customers,
       segment * 50 as segment_base
from segments
group by segment
order by segment, num_customers
limit 100;

Suggestion: [{'group': 'Constant_folding', 'produced_suggestion': 'Replace the two scalar subqueries computing d_month_seq+1 and d_month_seq+3 from June 2002 with the literal equivalent: d_year = 2002 AND d_moy BETWEEN 7 AND 9. Since d_month_seq is monotonically increasing per calendar month, the range June_month_seq+1..+3 is exactly July-September 2002, eliminating two date_dim scans (InitPlans).'}, {'group': 'Subquery_optimization', 'produced_suggestion': 'Deduplicate customer_sk early (before joining customer). Since c_current_addr_sk is functionally dependent on c_customer_sk, DISTINCT on both columns is equivalent to DISTINCT on customer_sk. Keep the UNION ALL of catalog_sales/web_sales before joining item and date_dim so the item and date joins are shared once (the previous rewrite pushed them into each UNION branch, causing regression), then join customer afterward to avoid sorting extra columns.'}, {'group': 'Predicate_simplification', 'produced_suggestion': 'Add the redundant-but-helpful predicate ca.ca_state IN (...) on customer_address. It is implied by ca_state = s_state AND s_state IN (...), but making it explicit enables early filtering of customer_address before the county/state join with store.'}, {'group': 'Query_optimization', 'produced_suggestion': 'Convert implicit comma joins to explicit JOIN syntax for better readability and clearer join-order hints to the optimizer; keep the aggregation pipeline (my_revenue -> segments -> final group by) unchanged as it is already minimal.'}]

Let's write in concise but detailed format.) then for each date, scanned sales. The new plan would scan date_dim using idx_date_dim_year_moy (cost maybe 60) to get 30 rows, materialize, then join to sales. But how does it join? It could do a nested loop from target_dates to sales using index on sold_date. That's exactly the same as original but with cheaper date_dim scan. The original already does nested loop from date_dim to sales, but it scanned date_dim via pk. So forcing the year/moy index should reduce cost by ~3000. That's a good optimization.

Can we do this without MATERIALIZED? Use a subquery with `OFFSET 0`? Not needed. We can write the query to filter date_dim first in a CTE. But we must ensure PostgreSQL uses the index. In PostgreSQL, a CTE `WITH target_dates AS (SELECT d_date_sk FROM date_dim WHERE d_year = 2002 AND d_moy = 6)` might be inlined, and planner might still choose the same plan. To force materialization, use `MATERIALIZED` keyword (PostgreSQL 12+). The explain is from PostgreSQL (cost, InitPlan, etc.), so version likely 12+. We can use `MATERIALIZED`. However, using MATERIALIZED might prevent predicate pushdown, but here it's a filter, so it's fine. It will materialize 30 rows, which is cheap.

Alternatively, we can use `AND cs.cs_sold_date_sk IN (SELECT d_date_sk FROM date_dim WHERE d_year=2002 AND d_moy=6)`. This might create a semi-join and use the index. Let's consider:
```
SELECT 1
FROM catalog_sales cs
JOIN item i ON cs.cs_item_sk = i.i_item_sk
WHERE cs.cs_bill_customer_sk = c.c_customer_sk
  AND i.i_category = 'Electronics'
  AND i.i_class = 'portable'
  AND cs.cs_wholesale_cost BETWEEN 70 AND 100
  AND cs.cs_sold_date_sk IN (SELECT d_date_sk FROM date_dim WHERE d_year=2002 AND d_moy=6)
```
This avoids joining date_dim directly. The planner can use a hash semi-join or nested loop. It will likely scan date_dim using idx_date_dim_year_moy to build a hash of 30 rows, then scan catalog_sales via index on customer? Wait, the filter is on customer in the exists. The outer query is customer. For each customer, it checks if there is a catalog_sales row with that customer and conditions. The planner might choose to scan catalog_sales by customer index (idx_catalog_sales_bill_customer_sk?) Does that index exist? The explain shows `idx_customer_sk` on customer, but for catalog_sales it used sold_date index. There is no index on cs_bill_customer_sk? The original query used `c_customer_sk = cs_or_ws_sales.customer_sk` after scanning sales by date. If we swap to exists from customer, the planner would need an index on cs_bill_customer_sk to be efficient. Does it exist? The explain for my_revenue shows `Bitmap Index Scan on idx_store_sales_customer` for store_sales, so there is an index on store_sales customer. For catalog_sales, the original plan didn't use a customer index because it started from date. There might be an index on cs_bill_customer_sk. In TPC-DS, indexes are usually created on foreign keys. The data_statistics show no index info, but explain mentions `idx_catalog_sales_sold_date`, `idx_web_sales_sold_date`, `idx_customer_sk`, `idx_item_category`, `idx_store_state`, `idx_store_sales_customer`, `idx_date_dim_year_moy`. WITH target_dates AS MATERIALIZED (

my_customers AS (

    UNION ALL

  JOIN target_dates d ON s.sold_date_sk = d.d_date_sk

  WHERE i.i_category = 'Electronics'

)

Worker 1: Found optimized SQL
. But the original explain shows Index Scan using idx_date_dim_sk on date_dim (cost=0.29..0.32 rows=1) for each store_sales row. That's already very cheap (0.03 cost per row). The number of store_sales rows per customer is estimated 89. So index lookups are fine. Changing to hash join might not be necessary. But it's an option. We can leave as is.

We should also consider adding an index on `date_dim (d_month_seq, d_date_sk)` to support the range filter? But it's small.

Let's think about the best rewrite score. We can present strategies:
1. Force date_dim filtering with MATERIALIZED CTE for target dates: Score 8/10. Reduces date_dim scan cost.
2. Replace scalar subqueries for month_seq with CTE: Score 6/10. Improves readability and avoids duplicate initplans.
3. Use explicit JOIN syntax: Score 5/10. Improves readability and allows better optimizer transformation.
4. Consider indexes: date_dim (d_year, d_moy) already exists; ensure catalog_sales/web_sales have sold_date indexes; store_sales customer index exists. Score 7/10 if missing.
5. Inline simple CTEs (segments): Score 4/10.

Most efficient way: Rewrite with MATERIALIZED target_dates, base_month CTE, explicit joins, and inline segments.

Now, final answer should include the optimized SQL. We need ensure it's executable. Let's test syntax.

Full SQL:
```
WITH target_dates AS MATERIALIZED (
    SELECT d_date_sk
    FROM date_dim
    WHERE d_year = 2002 AND d_moy = 6
),
my_customers AS (
    SELECT DISTINCT c.c_customer_sk, c.c_current_addr_sk
    FROM (
        SELECT cs_sold_date_sk AS sold_date_sk,
               cs_bill_customer_sk AS customer_sk,
               cs_item_sk AS item_sk,
               cs_wholesale_cost AS wholesale_cost
        FROM catalog_sales
        UNION ALL
        SELECT ws_sold_date_sk,
               ws_bill_customer_sk,
               ws_item_sk,
               ws_wholesale_cost
        FROM web_sales
    ) s
    JOIN target_dates d ON s.sold_date_sk = d.d_date_sk
    JOIN item i ON s.item_sk = i.i_item_sk
    JOIN customer c ON c.c_customer_sk = s.customer_sk
    WHERE i.i_category = 'Electronics'
      AND i.i_class = 'portable'
      AND s.wholesale_cost BETWEEN 70 AND 100
      AND c.c_birth_year BETWEEN 1978 AND 1991
),
base_month AS (
    SELECT d_month_seq AS month_seq
    FROM date_dim
    WHERE d_year = 2002 AND d_moy = 6
    LIMIT 1
),
my_revenue AS (
    SELECT mc.c_customer_sk, SUM(ss.ss_ext_sales_price) AS revenue
    FROM my_customers mc
    JOIN customer_address ca ON mc.c_current_addr_sk = ca.ca_address_sk
    JOIN store s ON ca.ca_county = s.s_county AND ca.ca_state = s.s_state
    JOIN store_sales ss ON mc.c_customer_sk = ss.ss_customer_sk
    JOIN date_dim d ON ss.ss_sold_date_sk = d.d_date_sk
    CROSS JOIN base_month bm
    WHERE ss.ss_wholesale_cost BETWEEN 70 AND 100
      AND s.s_state IN ('AL','AR','FL','MN','MO','NC','NY','OK','PA','WI')
      AND d.d_month_seq BETWEEN bm.month_seq + 1 AND bm.month_seq + 3
    GROUP BY mc.c_customer_sk
)
SELECT CAST(revenue / 50 AS INT) AS segment,
       COUNT(*) AS num_customers,
       CAST(revenue / 50 AS INT) * 50 AS segment_base
FROM my_revenue
GROUP BY CAST(revenue / 50 AS INT)
ORDER BY segment, num_customers
LIMIT 100;
```
Wait, in the final SELECT, `revenue` is from my_revenue. But we are grouping by `CAST(revenue/50 AS INT)`. In PostgreSQL, you cannot use `revenue` in SELECT if not grouped or aggregated. Here `revenue` is the column from my_revenue. The query is:
```
SELECT CAST(revenue/50 AS INT) AS segment, COUNT(*) ...
FROM my_revenue
GROUP BY CAST(revenue/50 AS INT)
```
This is valid because the expression `CAST(revenue/50 AS INT)` is in GROUP BY, so `revenue` is not selected directly. However, `COUNT(*)` counts rows per segment. But `my_revenue` has one row per customer, with `revenue` sum per customer. So grouping by segment of revenue will group customers with same revenue segment. This is exactly the original: `segments` CTE selects segment per customer, then group by segment. So it's correct. The `segment_base` is `segment * 50`, which is the same as `CAST(revenue/50 AS INT) * 50`. In original, `segment*50` where segment is the grouped segment, so it's fine. We can also use the alias `segment` in ORDER BY. In PostgreSQL, you can use alias in ORDER BY, but not in GROUP BY. We used expression in GROUP BY. That's fine.

But there's a subtlety: `CAST(revenue/50 AS INT)` might be truncated toward zero. In original, `cast((revenue/50) as int)`. Same. However, if revenue is negative, truncation differs? But revenue is sum of sales prices, positive. So fine.

Another subtlety: Original `segments` CTE: `select cast((revenue/50) as int) as segment from my_revenue`. Then final: `group by segment`. The final `segment_base` is `segment*50`. So our query is exactly equivalent.

Now, check if `base_month` CTE is referenced correctly. We do `CROSS JOIN base_month bm` in my_revenue. `base_month` has one row. If no row (no date), cross join yields no rows, same as original scalar subquery returning null causing no match. So equivalent.

Check `target_dates` materialization. We use `JOIN target_dates d ON s.sold_date_sk = d.d_date_sk`. This replaces the original join to date_dim with d_moy/year filters. Original also had `date_dim` in my_customers. Our target_dates has only d_date_sk. That's correct.

One potential issue: The original my_customers also joined `date_dim` and filtered `d_moy=6 and d_year=2002`. Our target_dates does exactly that. Good.

Now, should we use `UNION ALL` in subquery `s` as before. Yes.

Now, let's consider if we can further optimize by replacing `SELECT DISTINCT` with `GROUP BY` or `EXISTS`. I'll stick with DISTINCT for safety.

What about the `store` join? It might be better to use `EXISTS` if we can prove uniqueness, but we can't. So keep.

```sql
WITH target_dates AS MATERIALIZED (
    SELECT d_date_sk
    FROM date_dim
    WHERE d_year = 2002 AND d_moy = 6
),
my_customers AS (
    SELECT DISTINCT c.c_customer_sk, c.c_current_addr_sk
    FROM (
        SELECT cs_sold_date_sk AS sold_date_sk,
               cs_bill_customer_sk AS customer_sk,
               cs_item_sk AS item_sk,
               cs_wholesale_cost AS wholesale_cost
        FROM catalog_sales
        UNION ALL
        SELECT ws_sold_date_sk,
               ws_bill_customer_sk,
               ws_item_sk,
               ws_wholesale_cost
        FROM web_sales
    ) s
    JOIN target_dates d ON s.sold_date_sk = d.d_date_sk
    JOIN item i ON s.item_sk = i.i_item_sk
    JOIN customer c ON c.c_customer_sk = s.customer_sk
    WHERE i.i_category = 'Electronics'
      AND i.i_class = 'portable'
      AND s.wholesale_cost BETWEEN 70 AND 100
      AND c.c_birth_year BETWEEN 1978 AND 1991
),
base_month AS (
    SELECT d_month_seq AS month_seq
    FROM date_dim
    WHERE d_year = 2002 AND d_moy = 6
    LIMIT 1
),
my_revenue AS (
    SELECT mc.c_customer_sk, SUM(ss.ss_ext_sales_price) AS revenue
    FROM my_customers mc
    JOIN customer_address ca ON mc.c_current_addr_sk = ca.ca_address_sk
    JOIN store s ON ca.ca_county = s.s_county AND ca.ca_state = s.s_state
    JOIN store_sales ss ON mc.c_customer_sk = ss.ss_customer_sk
    JOIN date_dim d ON ss.ss_sold_date_sk = d.d_date_sk
    CROSS JOIN base_month bm
    WHERE ss.ss_wholesale_cost BETWEEN 70 AND 100
      AND s.s_state IN ('AL','AR','FL','MN','MO','NC','NY','OK','PA','WI')
      AND d.d_month_seq BETWEEN bm.month_seq + 1 AND bm.month_seq + 3
    GROUP BY mc.c_customer_sk
)
SELECT CAST(revenue / 50 AS INT) AS segment,
       COUNT(*) AS num_customers,
       CAST(revenue / 50 AS INT) * 50 AS segment_base
FROM my_revenue
GROUP BY CAST(revenue / 50 AS INT)
ORDER BY segment, num_customers
LIMIT 100;
```

The dominant cost in the original plan (~20500 of ~21910) is the `my_customers` block, specifically the `Parallel Index Scan using idx_date_dim_sk on date_dim` with only a `Filter` on `d_year`/`d_moy` — i.e., a full index scan of 73,049 rows to find ~30 dates. Wrapping that predicate in a `MATERIALIZED` CTE (`target_dates`) forces use of `idx_date_dim_year_moy`, reducing the date scan to ~30 rows before probing `catalog_sales`/`web_sales` by `sold_date_sk` indexes.

The two InitPlan scalar subqueries for `d_month_seq` are redundant duplicates; since `d_month_seq` is constant for a given (year, month), a single-row `base_month` CTE (with `LIMIT 1`) computes the value once and folds the `+1`/`+3` arithmetic directly into the range predicate — equivalent and cleaner.

The `segments` CTE is a trivial projection and is inlined into the final aggregation (`GROUP BY CAST(revenue/50 AS INT)`), which is semantically identical to the original `GROUP BY segment`.

The `DISTINCT` in `my_customers` and the `store` join on `(s_county, s_state)` (without `ss_store_sk`) were intentionally preserved because `store` acts as a location filter and the result multiplication (if multiple stores share a county/state) is part of the original semantics. With `target_dates` materialized, the sales-side row count stays small so the `DISTINCT` sort is not a bottleneck; replacing it with an `EXISTS` semi-join driving from `customer` risks a worse plan if no `cs_bill_customer_sk`/`ws_bill_customer_sk` index exists.

Suggested supporting indexes (most already present): `date_dim(d_year, d_moy)`, `catalog_sales(cs_sold_date_sk)`, `web_sales(ws_sold_date_sk)`, `store_sales(ss_customer_sk)`, `item(i_category, i_class)`.

```sql
WITH target_dates AS MATERIALIZED (
    SELECT d_date_sk
    FROM date_dim
    WHERE d_year = 2002 AND d_moy = 6
),
my_customers AS (
    SELECT DISTINCT c.c_customer_sk, c.c_current_addr_sk
    FROM (
        SELECT cs_sold_date_sk AS sold_date_sk,
               cs_bill_customer_sk AS customer_sk,
               cs_item_sk AS item_sk,
               cs_wholesale_cost AS wholesale_cost
        FROM catalog_sales
        UNION ALL
        SELECT ws_sold_date_sk,
               ws_bill_customer_sk,
               ws_item_sk,
               ws_wholesale_cost
        FROM web_sales
    ) s
    JOIN target_dates d ON s.sold_date_sk = d.d_date_sk
    JOIN item i ON s.item_sk = i.i_item_sk
    JOIN customer c ON c.c_customer_sk = s.customer_sk
    WHERE i.i_category = 'Electronics'
      AND i.i_class = 'portable'
      AND s.wholesale_cost BETWEEN 70 AND 100
      AND c.c_birth_year BETWEEN 1978 AND 1991
),
base_month AS (
    SELECT d_month_seq AS month_seq
    FROM date_dim
    WHERE d_year = 2002 AND d_moy = 6
    LIMIT 1
),
my_revenue AS (
    SELECT mc.c_customer_sk, SUM(ss.ss_ext_sales_price) AS revenue
    FROM my_customers mc
    JOIN customer_address ca ON mc.c_current_addr_sk = ca.ca_address_sk
    JOIN store s ON ca.ca_county = s.s_county AND ca.ca_state = s.s_state
    JOIN store_sales ss ON mc.c_customer_sk = ss.ss_customer_sk
    JOIN date_dim d ON ss.ss_sold_date_sk = d.d_date_sk
    CROSS JOIN base_month bm
    WHERE ss.ss_wholesale_cost BETWEEN 70 AND 100
      AND s.s_state IN ('AL','AR','FL','MN','MO','NC','NY','OK','PA','WI')
      AND d.d_month_seq BETWEEN bm.month_seq + 1 AND bm.month_seq + 3
    GROUP BY mc.c_customer_sk
)
SELECT CAST(revenue / 50 AS INT) AS segment,
       COUNT(*) AS num_customers,
       CAST(revenue / 50 AS INT) * 50 AS segment_base
FROM my_revenue
GROUP BY CAST(revenue / 50 AS INT)
ORDER BY segment, num_customers
LIMIT 100;
```

</analysis>
Both rewrites preserve the original semantics, but statement 1 is the more effective rewrite for the following reasons:

1. **Predicate pushdown into UNION branches**: Statement 1 pushes the `item`, `date_dim` filters and the wholesale-cost range directly into each branch of the UNION ALL before computing the customer set. This lets the optimizer filter `catalog_sales` and `web_sales` early (ideally partition/segment pruning on date and item joins against small dimension tables), drastically reducing rows before the customer-level aggregation. Statement 0 performs the same joins after the UNION ALL, forcing the union of raw wide rows (with item/date sk columns) to be built first.

2. **Reduced projection width**: Statement 1 only selects `customer_sk` in the union branches, avoiding carrying `sold_date_sk`, `item_sk`, and `wholesale_cost` columns through the union — less memory/IO.

3. **DISTINCT replaced by GROUP BY**: Statement 1's `GROUP BY customer_sk` accomplishes the same deduplication as the original `SELECT DISTINCT` (c_customer_sk functionally determines c_current_addr_sk), while making the dedup intent explicit and enabling hash-aggregation-based dedup.

4. **Date bounds via MIN with CROSS JOIN**: Replacing the correlated scalar subqueries `(select distinct d_month_seq+1 ...)` with a single `date_bounds` CTE joined once avoids repeated subquery evaluation and is cleaner for the optimizer.

5. **Avoids over-materialization**: Unlike statement 0, which uses `MATERIALIZED` on `target_dates` (a hint that can block predicate pushdown of the year/month filters into the fact-table scans), statement 1 leaves all joins inline, giving the optimizer maximum freedom for join reordering and filter pushdown.

Statement 0 is also a correct rewrite, but its MATERIALIZED CTE on `date_dim` and post-UNION joins make it slightly less favorable on a large TPC-DS-style schema.

</analysis>

## Selected Rewritten SQL: WITH qualifying_customers AS (
    SELECT customer_sk
    FROM (
        SELECT cs.cs_bill_customer_sk AS customer_sk
        FROM catalog_sales cs
        JOIN item i ON i.i_item_sk = cs.cs_item_sk
        JOIN date_dim d ON d.d_date_sk = cs.cs_sold_date_sk
        WHERE i.i_category = 'Electronics'
          AND i.i_class = 'portable'
          AND cs.cs_wholesale_cost BETWEEN 70 AND 100
          AND d.d_year = 2002
          AND d.d_moy = 6
        UNION ALL
        SELECT ws.ws_bill_customer_sk AS customer_sk
        FROM web_sales ws
        JOIN item i ON i.i_item_sk = ws.ws_item_sk
        JOIN date_dim d ON d.d_date_sk = ws.ws_sold_date_sk
        WHERE i.i_category = 'Electronics'
          AND i.i_class = 'portable'
          AND ws.ws_wholesale_cost BETWEEN 70 AND 100
          AND d.d_year = 2002
          AND d.d_moy = 6
    ) s
    GROUP BY customer_sk
),
my_customers AS (
    SELECT c.c_customer_sk, c.c_current_addr_sk
    FROM customer c
    JOIN qualifying_customers qc
      ON qc.customer_sk = c.c_customer_sk
    WHERE c.c_birth_year BETWEEN 1978 AND 1991
),
date_bounds AS (
    SELECT MIN(d_month_seq) + 1 AS start_month_seq,
           MIN(d_month_seq) + 3 AS end_month_seq
    FROM date_dim
    WHERE d_year = 2002
      AND d_moy = 6
),
my_revenue AS (
    SELECT c.c_customer_sk,
           SUM(ss.ss_ext_sales_price) AS revenue
    FROM my_customers c
    JOIN store_sales ss
      ON ss.ss_customer_sk = c.c_customer_sk
    JOIN customer_address ca
      ON ca.ca_address_sk = c.c_current_addr_sk
    JOIN store s
      ON s.s_county = ca.ca_county
     AND s.s_state = ca.ca_state
    JOIN date_dim d
      ON d.d_date_sk = ss.ss_sold_date_sk
    CROSS JOIN date_bounds db
    WHERE ss.ss_wholesale_cost BETWEEN 70 AND 100
      AND s.s_state IN ('AL','AR','FL','MN','MO','NC','NY','OK','PA','WI')
      AND d.d_month_seq BETWEEN db.start_month_seq AND db.end_month_seq
    GROUP BY c.c_customer_sk
),
segments AS (
    SELECT CAST(revenue / 50 AS int) AS segment
    FROM my_revenue
)
SELECT segment,
       COUNT(*) AS num_customers,
       segment * 50 AS segment_base
FROM segments
GROUP BY segment
ORDER BY segment, num_customers

Comparing the three EXPLAIN outputs, the original plan has a total cost of 21,910.85, the rewritten plan raises it to 29,591.30 (+35.1%), and the enhanced plan is byte-for-byte identical to the rewritten plan, meaning the enhancement pass produced no further planner-level change. The cost regression is concentrated in the fact-fetching subtree: the original's dominant node was the parallel Gather feeding the customer-dedup Unique (cost 20,512), which in the rewritten/enhanced plan becomes a Parallel Append costing 28,407.90 — a ~38.5% increase at the most expensive node. Structurally, however, the rewritten plan is notably cleaner: the two InitPlans (HashAggregates over date_dim for month-sequence bounds) are collapsed into a single Aggregate with a Join Filter; the Hash Join against item is replaced by a Nested Loop with a direct index lookup on idx_item_sk (eliminating the Bitmap Heap/Bitmap Index Scan pair and its hash build); the Unique-over-Sort dedup of customers is replaced by a lightweight Group node; the plain Append is upgraded to a Parallel Append; and the store_sales access flips from a Bitmap Heap Scan to a plain Index Scan. On the downside, the date_dim scan degrades from a Parallel Index Scan to a Parallel Seq Scan, and an additional Join Filter (ss.ss_customer_sk = cs.cs_bill_customer_sk) is introduced, which the optimizer cannot push below the nested loops. Memory-wise, the Hash node for the item build (≈107 rows) disappears, though two explicit Sort nodes remain at the top of the plan in all three variants. Worker count stays at 2 throughout. Net assessment: the rewrite trades ~35% estimated cost for plan simplification (fewer sort/dedup/hash operators, single-pass month bound computation), while the enhanced pass adds nothing beyond the rewrite.
</analysis>

</report>
1. Cost Efficiency
- Overall cost change: original 21,910.85 → rewritten 29,591.30, an increase of approximately 35.1%. The enhanced plan is identical to the rewritten plan (cost unchanged at 29,591.30), so the enhancement step yielded no measurable planner-level gain over the rewrite.
- Cost change at the most expensive plan node: the dominant subtree moved from the Gather node (cost 20,512.11 in the original) to the Parallel Append node (cost 28,407.90 in the rewritten/enhanced plan), an increase of roughly 38.5% at the hot spot. This is the primary contributor to the total cost regression.

2. Plan Characteristics
- Scan type transitions:
  - date_dim: Parallel Index Scan (original) → Parallel Seq Scan (rewritten/enhanced), a regression in access-path selectivity for the date filter (d_year = 2002, d_moy = 6).
  - item: Bitmap Heap Scan + Bitmap Index Scan (original) → direct Index Scan via idx_item_sk (rewritten/enhanced), removing the bitmap machinery but changing the join strategy.
  - store_sales: Bitmap Heap Scan + Bitmap Index Scan (original) → plain Index Scan on idx_store_sales_customer (rewritten/enhanced).
  - All other dimension lookups (customer, customer_address, store, catalog_sales, web_sales) remain Index Scans across all three plans.
- Join algorithm refinements:
  - The Hash Join on item (Hash Cond: cs_item_sk = i_item_sk) in the original is replaced by a Nested Loop with an index probe on item in the rewritten plan — appropriate for the very small estimated row counts, and it removes the hash-build step entirely.
  - The original's Unique-over-Sort customer deduplication is replaced by a much cheaper Group node over cs_bill_customer_sk in the rewritten plan.
  - The two InitPlans computing month-sequence bounds are consolidated into a single Aggregate node joined via a Join Filter, eliminating redundant date-dimension aggregation.
- Sort and intermediate result elimination:
  - The explicit Sort under the original Unique node is gone, removing one intermediate sort/dedup stage over the parallel subtree.
  - The rewritten plan's month-bound logic no longer materializes two separate HashAggregate result sets (InitPlan 1 and InitPlan 2), reducing intermediate result materialization.
  - Two explicit Sort nodes at the top of the plan (for the GroupAggregate and Incremental Sort) remain unchanged in all three variants.
- Introduced overhead: a new Join Filter (ss.ss_customer_sk = cs.cs_bill_customer_sk) in the rewritten plan cannot be pushed into an index condition and is evaluated as a residual filter.

3. Resource Utilization
- Memory: the Hash node for the item side of the original Hash Join (≈107 rows batched in memory) is eliminated, reducing per-worker hash-table memory. No new hash nodes are introduced in the rewritten/enhanced plans. Buffer-level behavior otherwise follows the scan-type changes above (fewer bitmap pages to recheck, but a full sequential scan on date_dim instead of an index traversal).
- Workers: the parallel worker count is unchanged at 2 across all three plans. The parallel section is restructured — the original used a parallel Nested Loop with a plain Append over catalog_sales/web_sales, while the rewritten/enhanced plans use a Parallel Append whose two arms (catalog branch and web branch) can be distributed across workers, potentially improving parallel balance even at a higher estimated cost.

4. Other Improvements
- Plan simplicity: the rewritten plan removes one full aggregation sub-plan (merging the duplicated date-dimension InitPlans), one Bitmap scan pair, one Hash build, and one Sort/Unique stage, producing a shallower and more predictable operator tree.
- Structural robustness: replacing the heuristic Unique dedup with a Group node and switching item access to a single-key index probe makes the plan less dependent on bitmap-selectivity estimates, which may improve stability in production despite the higher estimated cost.
- Enhancement pass outcome: the enhanced plan did not alter the rewritten plan (identical node tree, identical costs), indicating the rewriter already reached the planner's fixed point for this query; remaining improvement opportunities are at the physical-design level — e.g., a composite index on date_dim (d_year, d_moy) that would convert the Parallel Seq Scan back to an index scan, and an index supporting the ss/cs customer correlation to avoid the residual Join Filter.
- Summary recommendation: accept the rewritten/enhanced plan for its structural cleanliness, but monitor actual runtime; the 35% estimated cost increase is dominated by the sales-fact subtree, so validating with real execution statistics (buffers/timing) is advised before promotion.
</report>

Now, we need to present the final answer. The user asked: "You are an experienced DBA, and your mission is to perform high-quality query rewriting for the user... Identify potential bottlenecks... assign rewrite score... determine most efficient... Rewrite SQL if necessary... Notably, you will be provided with previous rewrite report... If you find SQL already well-optimized... include TERMINATE."

Since we are providing a new rewrite, we should not include TERMINATE. We should explain the strategies and scores.

Let's structure the response:
- Summary of analysis: The main bottleneck is the my_customers CTE, particularly the DISTINCT over a join including customer, and the repeated scalar subqueries for month bounds. The previous rewrite regressed because it pushed item/date joins into each UNION ALL branch and used date_bounds CTE. Our strategy: keep UNION ALL of sales before joining item/date, deduplicate only customer_sk early, join customer after, replace scalar subqueries with literal month range, add explicit state filter on customer_address, use explicit joins.
- Rewrite scores for strategies:
  1. Replace repeated scalar subqueries with literal month range: Score 5/5 (eliminates InitPlans and simplifies).
  2. Deduplicate customer_sk before joining customer: Score 4/5 (avoids sorting extra columns, reduces join size).
  3. Keep UNION ALL before item/date joins: Score 5/5 (enables shared hash joins, avoids duplicate work).
  4. Add explicit `ca.ca_state IN (...)` filter: Score 3/5 (helps early filter).
  5. Use explicit JOIN syntax: Score 2/5 (readability, minor optimizer benefits).
- Most efficient way: the combination of 1,2,3,4 in the rewritten SQL.
- Provide rewritten SQL.
- Conclude that this rewrite should improve performance.

We should also mention that the rewritten SQL is executable and equivalent.

Let's write the final answer.

 70 and 100
),
my_customers as (
    select c.c_customer_sk, c.c_current_addr_sk
    from customer c
    join qualifying_customers qc on qc.customer_sk = c.c_customer_sk
    where c.c_birth_year between 1978 and 1991
),
my_revenue as (
    select c.c_customer_sk,
           sum(ss.ss_ext_sales_price) as revenue
    from my_customers c
    join store_sales ss on ss.ss_customer_sk = c.c_customer_sk
    join customer_address ca on ca.ca_address_sk = c.c_current_addr_sk
    join store s on s.s_county = ca.ca_county and s.s_state = ca.ca_state
    join date_dim d on d.d_date_sk = ss.ss_sold_date_sk
    where ss.ss_wholesale_cost between 70 and 100
      and s.s_state in ('AL','AR','FL','MN','MO','NC','NY','OK','PA','WI')
      and ca.ca_state in ('AL','AR','FL','MN','MO','NC','NY','OK','PA','WI')
      and d.d_year = 2002
      and d.d_moy between 7 and 9
    group by c.c_customer_sk
),
segments as (
    select cast(revenue / 50 as int) as segment
    from my_revenue
)
select segment,
       count(*) as num_customers,
       segment * 50 as segment_base
from segments
group by segment
order by segment, num_customers
limit 100;
```

<analysis>
Enhanced SQL 0 is a faithful rewrite: it replaces the scalar subquery date range (d_month_seq+1 to +3 for 2002-06) with the equivalent direct predicate d_year=2002 and d_moy between 7 and 9, preserving semantics while removing an expensive correlated subquery evaluation. It keeps the original join structure and distinct behavior intact.

Enhanced SQL 1 introduces an extra filter `ca.ca_state in (...)` not present in the original. While logically it might seem implied (since ca_state = s_state and s_state in the list), adding it changes the plan's filter pushdown but is semantically safe. However, it restructures my_customers to drop DISTINCT and uses explicit joins — this is fine since the join to qualifying_customers is on the unique customer_sk. But rewriting the CTE chain changes the my_customers definition (no distinct on c_current_addr_sk), which is semantically equivalent for the final revenue aggregation since c_customer_sk uniqueness suffices for grouping.

Both are valid, but SQL 1's restructuring with explicit joins and early projection/filter pushdown (qualifying_customers computed before joining customer) gives the optimizer a cleaner, smaller driving set and explicit join ordering. The added ca_state filter further prunes customer_address rows early. SQL 1 offers better potential performance while remaining semantically equivalent.
</analysis>

## Selected Rewritten SQL: with all_sales as (
    select cs_sold_date_sk as sold_date_sk,

),

    from all_sales s
    join item i on i.i_item_sk = s.item_sk

),

Comparing the three EXPLAIN outputs:

1. **Overall cost**: The original plan's top-level Limit cost is 21,910.89. The rewritten plan reduces this to 20,634.54, and the enhanced plan is identical to the rewritten plan (20,634.54). This is a reduction of approximately **5.8%** (≈1,276.35 cost units).

2. **Most expensive node**: The costliest node in the original is the Subquery Scan / inner Nested Loop subtree (cost reaching 21,910.80 / 21,790.43). In the rewritten/enhanced plans, the corresponding nodes drop to 20,634.44 / 20,634.41, a **~5.3–5.8% reduction** at the most expensive plan node.

3. **Structural changes driving the improvement**:
   - The original plan builds two **InitPlans** (HashAggregate over date_dim with Index Scan, cost ~60 each) to compute month-sequence boundaries, then applies them as a filter on the date_dim join. The rewritten plan eliminates both InitPlans entirely, replacing them with a direct predicate pushdown (`d_moy >= 7 AND d_moy <= 9 AND d_year = 2002`) on the final date_dim Index Scan — removing two HashAggregate nodes and simplifying the filter logic.
   - The `store_sales` access path changes from a **Bitmap Heap Scan + Bitmap Index Scan** (cost 6.86..1268.49, rows=320 before filter) to a direct **Index Scan** (cost 0.44..103.93, rows=89), which is roughly **8x cheaper** at that node and avoids bitmap build/recheck overhead.
   - The `Unique` node in the original de-duplicates on two columns (c_customer_sk, c_current_addr_sk) with a two-key Sort; the rewritten version de-duplicates only on `cs_bill_customer_sk` (single-key Sort, width 4 instead of 8), producing a narrower intermediate result.
   - The `customer_address` Index Scan drops from cost 8.44 to 0.84 because the state-list filter is pushed into the index scan path.
   - A minor semantic difference: the store_sales-to-catalog_sales correlation now uses an explicit **Join Filter** (`ss.ss_customer_sk = catalog_sales.cs_bill_customer_sk`) rather than an InitPlan-based range predicate.

4. **Unchanged elements**: The parallel subtree (Gather with 2 workers, Hash Join against item's Bitmap Heap Scan, Parallel Index Scan on date_dim, Append over catalog_sales/web_sales) is identical in all three plans, as are the final GroupAggregate/Sort/Incremental Sort stages and the Limit.

5. **Rewritten vs. enhanced**: The enhanced EXPLAIN is byte-for-byte identical to the rewritten EXPLAIN — the enhancement pass produced no additional plan changes, indicating the rewrite already converged to the planner's optimal choice.

**Query Optimization Analysis Report**

**1. Cost Efficiency**
- **Overall cost change**: Total plan cost decreased from 21,910.89 (original) to 20,634.54 (rewritten/enhanced), a **reduction of ~5.8%** (about 1,276 cost units). The enhanced plan matches the rewritten plan exactly, confirming the rewrite achieved the full available benefit.
- **Most expensive node**: The dominant cost node (the Subquery Scan wrapping the deep Nested Loop chain) fell from 21,910.80 to 20,634.44 (**-5.8%**), while the innermost Nested Loop dropped from 21,790.43 to 20,634.41 (**-5.3%**). The improvement propagates from structural simplifications at the bottom of the plan.

**2. Plan Characteristics**
- **Scan type transitions**:
  - `store_sales`: Bitmap Heap Scan (with Bitmap Index Scan) → direct **Index Scan** on `idx_store_sales_customer`, cutting node cost from 1,268.49 to 103.93 and reducing pre-filter row estimates from 320 to 89.
  - `customer_address`: cost per lookup reduced from 8.44 to 0.84 by pushing the state-list filter down to the index scan.
- **Join algorithm refinements**: The core join structure (Nested Loop chain over a parallel Hash Join) is preserved, which is appropriate given the low row estimates. The store_sales correlation was converted from an InitPlan-range lookup into a direct Join Filter against catalog_sales, removing indirection.
- **Elimination of explicit sorting and intermediate results**:
  - Two **InitPlans with HashAggregate nodes** (month-sequence computation over date_dim) were completely eliminated, replaced by inline predicate pushdown (`d_moy BETWEEN 7 AND 9 AND d_year = 2002`) on the final date_dim Index Scan — removing both hash aggregation work and the correlated range filter evaluation.
  - The `Unique` de-duplication key was narrowed from two columns (c_customer_sk + c_current_addr_sk, width 8) to one column (cs_bill_customer_sk, width 4), shrinking the sort input and intermediate result width.
- The plan retains its incremental-sort-friendly top structure (GroupAggregate → Sort → Incremental Sort), which remains efficient.

**3. Resource Utilization**
- **Memory**: Removing the two HashAggregate InitPlans and converting store_sales from Bitmap Heap Scan to Index Scan reduces hash/bitmap memory pressure. The item-side Hash build (~107 rows) is unchanged and remains cheap.
- **Workers**: Parallelism is unchanged at **2 workers** (Gather node), so the cost savings come purely from structural simplification rather than added parallelism — an efficient outcome since no additional CPU/memory resources are consumed.
- Buffer access is reduced via the store_sales Index Scan (no heap bitmap recheck pass) and the tighter customer_address scans.

**4. Other Improvements**
- **Predicate pushdown**: The date-range condition previously expressed via InitPlans is now fused directly into the date_dim index scan filter, improving both clarity and execution efficiency.
- **Narrower tuples** flow through the upper plan nodes (e.g., the Unique/Sort stage), reducing sort memory and comparison overhead.
- **Rewritten vs. enhanced plan**: identical in every node, cost, and row estimate — the rewrite is already optimal; the enhancement step validated convergence rather than contributing further gains.
- All selectivity-critical filters (wholesale cost bounds, birth-year range, county match) remain in place, so the cost reduction entails no semantic loosening of the query's filtering logic.
</report>
